Types and Terms Translated: Unrestricted Resources in Encoding Functions as Processes
Joseph W. N. Paulus, Daniele Nantes-Sobrinho, Jorge A. Pérez

Abstract
Type-preserving translations are effective rigorous tools in the study of core programming calculi. In this paper, we develop a new typed translation that connects sequential and concurrent calculi; it is governed by type systems that control resource consumption . Our main contribution is the source language, a new resource λ \lambda -calculus with non-determinism and failures, dubbed u ​ λ ⊕ ↯ \mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus} . In u ​ λ ⊕ ↯ \mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus} , resources are split into linear and unrestricted; failures are explicit and arise from this distinction. We define a type system based on intersection types to control resources and fail-prone computation. The target language is 𝗌 ​ π \mathsf{s}\pi , an existing session-typed π \pi -calculus that results from a Curry-Howard correspondence between linear logic and session types. Our typed translation subsumes our prior work; interestingly, it treats unrestricted resources in u ​ λ ⊕ ↯ \mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus} as client-server session behaviours in 𝗌 ​ π \mathsf{s}\pi .

1
Introduction

Context

Type-preserving translations
are effective rigorous tools in the study of core programming calculi.
They can be seen as an abstract counterpart to the type-preserving compilers that enable key optimisations in the implementation of programming languages.
The goal of this paper is to develop a new typed translation that connects sequential and concurrent calculi, and is governed by type systems that control
resource consumption
.

A central idea in the resource
\lambda
-calculus is to consider that in an application
M\,N
the argument
N
is a
resource
of possibly limited availability.
This generalisation of the
\lambda
-calculus triggers many fascinating questions, such as typability, solvability, expressiveness power, etc., which have been studied in different settings (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
16
,
7
]
).
In established resource
\lambda
-calculi, such as those by Boudol
[
1
]
and by Pagani and Ronchi della Rocca
[
16
]
, a more general form of application is considered: a term can be applied to a bag of resources
B=\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\ldots\cdot\lbag N_{k}\rbag
, where
N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}
denote terms; then, an application
M\ B
must take into account that each
N_{i}
may be reusable or not. Thus, non-determinism is natural in resource
\lambda
-calculi, because a term has now multiple ways of consuming resources from the bag. This bears a strong resemblance with process calculi such as the
\pi
-calculus
[
15
]
, in which concurrent interactions are intrinsically non-deterministic.

There are different flavors of non-determinism. Over two decades ago, Boudol and Laneve
[
2
,
3
]
explored connections between a resource
\lambda
-calculus and the
\pi
-calculus. In their setting, an application
M\ B
would branch, i.e.,
M
could consume a resource
N_{j}
in
B
(with
j\in\{1,\ldots k\}
) and discard the other
k-1
resources in a non-confluent manner; this is what we call a
collapsing
approach to non-determinism.
On a different direction, Pagani and Ronchi della Rocca
[
16
]
proposed
\lambda^{r}
, a resource
\lambda
-calculus that implements
non-collapsing
non-determinism, whereby all the possible alternatives for resource consumption are retained together in a sum, ensuring confluence. They investigated typability and characterisations of solvability in
\lambda^{r}
, but no connection with the
\pi
-calculus was established. In an attempt to address this gap, our previous work
[
17
]
identified
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a resource
\lambda
-calculus with non-collapsing non-determinism, explicit failure, and
linear
resources (to be used exactly once), and developed a correct typed translation into a session typed
\pi
-calculus
[
5
]
. The calculus
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, however, does not include
unrestricted
resources (to be used zero or many times).

This Paper

Here we introduce a new
\lambda
-calculus, dubbed
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, its intersection type system, and its translation into session-typed processes.
Our motivation is twofold: to elucidate the status of unrestricted resources in a functional setting with non-collapsing non-determinism, and to characterise unrestricted resources within a translation of functions into processes.
Unlike its predecessors,
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
distinguishes between linear and unrestricted resources. This distinction determines the semantics of terms and especially the deadlocks (
failures
) that arise due to mismatches in resources. This way,
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
subsumes
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, which is purely linear and cannot express failures related to unrestricted resources.

Distinguishing linear and unrestricted resources is not a new insight. This idea goes back to Boudol’s
\lambda
-calculus with multiplicities
[
1
]
, where arguments can be tagged as unrestricted. What is new about
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is that the distinction between linear and unrestricted resources leads to two main differences.
First, occurrences of a variable can be linear or unrestricted, depending on the kind of resources they should be substituted with. This way, e.g., a linear occurrence of variable must be substituted with a linear resource.
In
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a variable can have linear and unrestricted occurrences in the same term.
(Notice that we use the adjective ‘linear’ in connection to resources used exactly once, and not to the number of occurrences of a variable in a term.)
Second, failures depend on the nature of the involved resource(s). In
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, a linear failure arises from a mismatch between required and available (linear) resources; an unrestricted failure arises when a specific (unrestricted) resource is not available.

Accordingly, the syntax of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
incorporates linear and unrestricted resources, enabling their consistent separation, within non-collapsing non-determinism.
The calculus allows for linear and unrestricted occurrences of variables, as just discussed;
bags comprise two separate zones, linear and unrestricted; and the
failure term

\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}}
explicitly mentions the linear variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
The (lazy) reduction semantics of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
includes two different rules for “fetching” terms from bags, and for consistently handling the failure term.

We equip
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with non-idempotent intersection types, extending the approach in
[
17
]
: in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, intersection types account for more than resource multiplicity, since the elements of the unrestricted bag can have different types. Using intersection types, we define a class of
well-formed

\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions, which includes terms that correctly consume resources but also terms that may reduce to the failure term. Well-formed expressions thus subsume the
well-typed
expressions that can be defined in a sub-language of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
without the failure term.

The calculus
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
can express terms whose dynamic behaviour is not captured by prior works. This way, e.g., the identity function
{\bf I}
admits two formulations, depending on whether the variable occurrence is linear or unrestricted.
One can have
\lambda x.x
, as usual, but also the unrestricted variant
\lambda x.x[i]
, where ‘
[i]
’ is an index annotation (similar to a qualifier or a tag), which indicates that
x
should be replaced by the
i
-th element of the unrestricted zone of the bag. The behaviour of these functions will depend on the bags that are provided as their arguments.
Similarly, we can express variants of
\Delta=\lambda x.xx
and
\Omega=\Delta\,\Delta
whose behaviours again depend on linear or unrestricted occurrences of variables and bags.
Consider the term
\Delta_{7}=\lambda x.(x[1](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[2]\rbag^{!}))
,
where we use ‘
\star
’ to separate linear and unrestricted resources in the bag, and ‘
\diamond
’ denotes concatenation of unrestricted resources.
Term
\Delta_{7}
is an abstraction on
x
of an application of an unrestricted occurrence of
x
, which aims to consume the first component of an unrestricted bag, to a bag with an empty linear zone (denoted
\mathtt{1}
) and an unrestricted zone with resources
\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}
and
\lbag x[2]\rbag^{!}
. The self-application
\Delta_{7}\Delta_{7}
produces a non-terminating behaviour and yet
\Delta_{7}
itself is well-formed (see Example
16
).

Both
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
are
logically motivated
resource
\lambda
-calculi, in the following sense: their design has been strongly influenced by
\mathsf{s}\pi
, a typed
\pi
-calculus resulting from the Curry-Howard correspondence between linear logic and session types in
[
5
]
, where proofs correspond to processes and cut elimination to process communication.
As demonstrated in
[
5
]
, providing primitive support for explicit failures is key to expressing many useful programming idioms (such as exceptions); this insight is a leading motivation in our design for
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

To attest to the logical underpinnings of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, we develop a typed translation (or
encoding
) of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
and establish its correctness with respect to well-established criteria
[
9
,
14
]
.
As in
[
17
]
, we encode
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
by relying on an intermediate language with
sharing
constructs
[
10
,
8
,
13
]
.
A key idea in encoding
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is to codify the behaviour of unrestricted occurrences of a variable and their corresponding resources in the bag as
client-server connections
, leveraging the copying semantics for the exponential “
!A
” induced by the Curry-Howard correspondence.
This typed encoding into
\mathsf{s}\pi
justifies the semantics of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
in terms of precise session protocols (i.e., linear logic propositions, because of the correspondence).

In summary, the
main contributions
of this paper are:
(1) The resource calculus
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
of linear and unrestricted resources, and its associated intersection type system. (2) A typed encoding of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, which connects well-formed expressions (disciplined by intersection types) and well-typed concurrent processes (disciplined by session types, under the Curry-Howard correspondence with linear logic), subsuming the results in
[
17
]
.

Additional Material

The appendices contain omitted material.
App.
A
collects technical details on
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
App.
B
details the proof of subject reduction for well-formed
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions. App.
C
–App.
F
collect omitted definitions and proofs for our encoding of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

2
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
: Unrestricted Resources, Non-Determinism, and Failure

Syntax.

We shall use
x,y,\ldots
to range over
variables
, and
i,j\ldots
, as positive integers, to range over
indices
.
Variable occurrences will be
annotated
to distinguish the kind of resource they should be substituted with (linear or unrestricted).
With a slight abuse of terminology, we may write ‘linear variable’ and ‘unrestricted variable’ to refer to linear and unrestricted occurrences of a variable.
As we will see, a variable’s annotation will be inconsequential for binding purposes.
We write
{\widetilde{x}}
to abbreviate
{x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{n}
, for
n\geq 1
and each
x_{i}
distinct.

Definition 1

(
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

We define
terms
(
M,N
),
bags
(
A,B
), and
expressions
(
\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N}
) as:

(Annotations)

\displaystyle[*]

\displaystyle::=[i]\ |\ [{\ell}]\qquad i\in\mathbb{N}

(Terms)

\displaystyle M,N

\displaystyle::=x[*]\ |\ \lambda x.M\ |\ (M\ B)\ |\ M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\ |\ \mathtt{fail}^{{\widetilde{x}}}

(Linear Bags)

\displaystyle C,D

\displaystyle::=\mathtt{1}\ |\ \lbag M\rbag\cdot\,C

(Unrestricted Bags)

\displaystyle U,V

\displaystyle::=\mathtt{1}^{!}\ |\ \lbag M\rbag^{!}\ |\ U\diamond V

(Bags)

\displaystyle A,B

\displaystyle::=C\star U

(Expressions)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle::=M\ |\ \mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}

To lighten up notation, we shall omit the annotation for linear variables. This way, e.g., we write
(\lambda x.x)B

rather than
(\lambda x.x[{\ell}])B
.

\Cref

def:rsyntaxfailunres introduces three syntactic categories:
terms
(in functional position);
bags
(multisets of resources, in argument position), and
expressions
, which are finite formal sums that denote possible results of a computation. Below we describe each category in details.

•

Terms (unary expressions):

–

Variables: We write
x[{\ell}]
to denote a
linear
occurrence of
x
, i.e, an occurrence that can only be substituted for linear resources. Similarly,
x[i]
denotes an
unrestricted
occurrence of
x
, i.e., an occurrence that can only be substituted for a resource located at the
i
-th position of an unrestricted bag.

–

Abstractions
\lambda x.M
of a variable
x
in a term
M
, which may have contain linear or unrestricted occurrences of
x
.
This way, e.g.,
\lambda x.x
and
\lambda x.x[i]
are linear and unrestricted versions of the identity function.
Notice that the scope of
x
is
M
, as usual, and that
\lambda x.(\cdot)
binds both linear and unrestricted occurrences of
x
.

–

Applications of a term
M
to a bag
B
(written
M\ B
) and the explicit substitution of a bag
B
for a variable
x
(written
\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
) are as expected (cf.
[
1
,
3
]
).
Notice that in
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
the occurrences of
x
in
M
, linear and unrestricted, are bound.
Some conditions apply to
B
: this will be evident later on, after we define our operational semantics (cf. Fig.
1
).

–

The failure term
\mathtt{fail}^{{\widetilde{x}}}
denotes a term that will result from a reduction in which there is a lack or excess of resources, where
{\widetilde{x}}
denotes a multiset of free linear variables that are encapsulated within failure.

•

A bag
B
is defined as
C\star U
: the concatenation of a bag of linear resources
C
with a bag (actually, a list) of unrestricted resources
U
. We write
\lbag M\rbag
to denote the linear bag that encloses term
M
, and use
\lbag M\rbag^{!}
in the unrestricted case.

–

Linear bags (
C,D,\ldots
) are multisets of terms. The empty linear bag is denoted
\mathtt{1}
. We write
C_{1}\cdot C_{2}
to denote the concatenation of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
; this is a commutative and associative operation, where
\mathtt{1}
is the identity.

–

Unrestricted bags (
U,V,\ldots
) are ordered lists of terms.
The empty unrestricted bag is denoted as
\mathtt{1}^{!}
.
The concatenation of
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
is denoted by
U_{1}\diamond U_{2}
; this operation is associative but not commutative.
Given
i\geq 1
, we write
U_{i}
to denote the
i
-th element of the unrestricted (ordered) bag
U
.

•

Expressions are sums of terms, denoted as
\sum_{i}^{n}N_{i}
, where
n>0
. Sums are associative and commutative; reordering of the terms in a sum is performed silently.

Example 2
.

Consider the term

M:=\lambda x.(x[1]\lbag x\rbag\star\lbag y[1]\rbag^{!})
,
which has linear and unrestricted occurrences of the same variable.
This is an abstraction of an application that contains two bound occurrences of
x
(one unrestricted with index
1
, and one linear) and one free unrestricted occurrence of
y[1]
, occurring in an unrestricted bag.
As we will see, in
M\ (C\star U)
, the unrestricted occurrence ‘
x[1]
’ should be replaced by the first element of
U
.

The salient features of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
—the explicit construct for failure, the index annotations on unrestricted variables, the ordering of unrestricted bags—are
design choices
that will be responsible for interesting behaviours, as the following examples illustrate.

Example 3
.

As already mentioned,

\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
admits different variants of the usual
\lambda
-term
{\bf I}=\lambda x.x
. We could have one in which
x
is a linear variable (i.e.,
\lambda x.x
), but also several possibilities if
x
is unrestricted (i.e.,
\lambda x.x[i]
, for some positive integer
i
).
Interestingly, because
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
supports failures, non-determinism, and the consumption of arbitrary terms of the unrestricted bag, these two variants of
{\bf I}
can have behaviours that may differ from the usual interpretation of
{\bf I}
. In Example
9
we will show that the six terms below give different behaviours:

•

M_{1}=(\lambda x.x)(\lbag N\rbag\star U)

•

M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag N_{2}\rbag\star U)

•

M_{3}=(\lambda x.x[1])(\lbag N\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})

•

M_{4}=(\lambda x.x[1])(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag N\rbag^{!}\diamond U)

•

M_{5}=(\lambda x.x[1])(\mathtt{1}\star\mathtt{1}^{!}\diamond U)

•

M_{6}=(\lambda x.x[i])(C\diamond U)

We will see that
M_{1}
,
M_{4}
,
M_{6}
reduce without failures, whereas
M_{2}
,
M_{3}
,
M_{5}
reduce to failure.

Example 4
.

Similarly,
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
allows for several forms of the standard
\lambda
-terms such as
\Delta:=\lambda x.xx
and
\Omega:=\Delta\Delta
, depending on whether the variable
x
is linear or unrestricted:

1.

\Delta_{1}:=\lambda x.(x(\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}))
consists of an abstraction of a linear occurrence of
x
applied to a linear bag containing another linear occurrence of
x
. There are two forms of self-applications of
\Delta_{1}
, namely:
\Delta_{1}(\lbag\Delta_{1}\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})
and
\Delta_{1}(1\star\lbag\Delta_{1}\rbag^{!})
.

2.

\Delta_{4}:=\lambda x.(x[1](\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}))
consists of an unrestricted occurrence of
x
applied to a linear bag (containing a linear occurrence of
x
) that is composed with an empty unrestricted bag. Similarly, there are two self-applications of
\Delta_{4}
, namely:
\Delta_{4}(\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})
and
\Delta_{4}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!})
.

3.

We show applications of an unrestricted variable occurrence (
x[2]
or
x[1]
) applied to an empty linear bag composed with a non-empty unrestricted bag (of size two):

•

\Delta_{3}=\lambda x.(x[1](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}))

•

\Delta_{5}:=\lambda x.(x[2](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[2]\rbag^{!}))

•

\Delta_{6}:=\lambda x.(x[1](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[2]\rbag^{!}))

•

\Delta_{7}:=\lambda x.(x[2](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}))

Applications between these terms express behaviour, similar to a lazy evaluation of
\Omega
:

•

\Omega_{5}:=\Delta_{5}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{5}\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag\Delta_{5}\rbag^{!})

•

\Omega_{5,6}:=\Delta_{5}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{5}\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag\Delta_{6}\rbag^{!})

•

\Omega_{6,5}:=\Delta_{6}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{5}\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag\Delta_{6}\rbag^{!})

•

\Omega_{7}:=\Delta_{7}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{7}\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag\Delta_{7}\rbag^{!})

The behaviour of these terms will be made explicit later on (see Examples
11
and
12
).

Semantics.

The semantics of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
captures that linear resources can be used only once, and that unrestricted resources can be used
ad libitum
. Thus, the evaluation of a function applied to a multiset of linear resources produces
different possible behaviours, depending on the way these resources are substituted for the linear variables. This induces non-determinism, which we formalise using a
non-collapsing
approach, in which expressions keep all the different possibilities open, and do not commit to one of them. This is in contrast to
collapsing
non-determinism, in which selecting one alternative discards the rest.

We define a reduction relation
\longrightarrow
, which operates lazily on expressions. Informally, a
\beta
-reduction induces an explicit substitution of a bag
B=C\star U
for a variable
x
, denoted
\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, in a term
M
.
This explicit substitution is then expanded
depending on whether the head of
M

has a linear or an unrestricted variable.
Accordingly, in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
there are
two sources of failure
: one concerns mismatches on linear resources (required vs available resources); the other concerns the unavailability of a required unrestricted resource (an empty bag
\mathtt{1}^{!}
).

To formalise reduction, we require a few auxiliary notions.

Definition 5
.

The multiset of free linear variables of
\mathbb{M}
, denoted
\mathsf{mlfv}(\mathbb{M})
, is defined below.
We denote by
[{x}]
the multiset containing the linear variable
x
and
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
denotes the multiset containing
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. We write
\widetilde{x}\uplus\widetilde{y}
to denote the multiset union of
\widetilde{x}
, and
\widetilde{y}
and
\widetilde{x}\setminus y
to express that every occurrence of
y
is removed from
\widetilde{x}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}({x})

\displaystyle=[{x}]

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}({x}[i])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}({\mathtt{1}})=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}(C\star U)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}(C)

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}(M\ B)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}(M)\uplus\mathsf{mlfv}(B)

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}({\lbag M\rbag})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}(M)

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}(\lambda x.M)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}(M)\!\setminus\!\{{x}\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{mlfv}(M)\setminus\{{x}\})\uplus\mathsf{mlfv}(B)

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}({\lbag M\rbag}\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}({M})\uplus\mathsf{mlfv}(C)

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}(\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mlfv}(\mathbb{M})\uplus\mathsf{mlfv}(\mathbb{N})

\displaystyle\mathsf{mlfv}(\mathtt{fail}^{{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{n}})

\displaystyle=[{x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{n}]

A term
M
(resp. expression
\mathbb{M}
) is called
linearly closed
if
\mathsf{mlfv}(M)=\emptyset
(resp.
\mathsf{mlfv}(\mathbb{M})=\emptyset
).

Notation 1
.

We shall use the following notations.

•

N\in\mathbb{M}
means that

N
occurs in the sum
\mathbb{M}
.
Also, we write
N_{i}\in C
to denote that
N_{i}
occurs in the linear bag
C
, and
C\setminus N_{i}
to denote the linear bag obtained by removing one occurrence of
N_{i}
from
C
.

•

\#({x},M)
denotes the number of (free) linear occurrences of
x
in
M
.
Also,
\#(x,\widetilde{y})
denotes the number of occurrences of
x
in the multiset
\widetilde{y}
.

•

\mathsf{PER}(C)
is the set of all permutations of a linear bag
C
and
C_{i}(n)
denotes the
n
-th term in the (permuted)
C_{i}
.

•

\mathsf{size}(C)
denotes the number of terms in a linear bag
C
.
That is,
\mathsf{size}(\mathtt{1})=0

and

\mathsf{size}(\lbag M\rbag\cdot\,C)=1+\mathsf{size}(C)
. Given a bag
B=C\star U
, we define
\mathsf{size}(B)
as
\mathsf{size}(C)
.

Definition 6

(Head)
.

Given a term
M
, we define
\mathsf{head}(M)
inductively as:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{aligned} \mathsf{head}(x)&=x&\mathsf{head}(M\ B)&=\mathsf{head}(M)&\mathsf{head}(\lambda x.M)&=\lambda x.M\\
\mathsf{head}(x[i])&=x[i]&\mathsf{head}(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})&=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}&\mathsf{head}(M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)&=\begin{cases}\mathsf{head}(M)&\text{if $\#(x,M)=\mathsf{size}(B)$}\\
\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{aligned}\end{array}

Definition 7

(Head Substitution)
.

Let
M
be a term such that
\mathsf{head}(M)=x
.
The
head substitution
of a term
N
for
x
in
M
, denoted
M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}
, is inductively defined as follows (where
x\not=y
):

\displaystyle x{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}=N\hskip 5.69054pt(M\ B){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ B\quad(M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle){\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}=(M{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}})\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle

When
\mathsf{head}(M)=x[i]
, the head substitution
M{\{\!|}N/x[i]{|\!\}}
works as expected:
x[i]{\{\!|}N/x[i]{|\!\}}=N
as the base case of the definition.
Finally, we define contexts for terms and expressions:

Definition 8

(Evaluation Contexts)
.

Contexts for terms (CTerm) and expressions (CExpr) are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}l}\text{(CTerm)}\quad C[\cdot],C^{\prime}[\cdot]::=([\cdot])B\mid([\cdot])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\text{(CExpr)}\quad D[\cdot],D^{\prime}[\cdot]::=M+[\cdot]\end{array}

\mathtt{[R:Beta]}

(\lambda x.M)B\longrightarrow M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\mathsf{head}(M)={x}

C={\lbag N_{1}\rbag}\cdot\dots\cdot{\lbag N_{k}\rbag}\ ,\ k\geq 1

\#({x},M)=k

\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{\ell}]}

M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow M{\{\!|}N_{1}/{x}{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$(C\setminus N_{1})\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle+\cdots+M{\{\!|}N_{k}/{x}{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$(C\setminus N_{k})\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\mathsf{head}(M)={x}[i]\hskip 9.24994pt\#({x},M)=\mathsf{size}(C)

U_{i}=\lbag N\rbag^{!}

\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{!}]}

M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow M{\{\!|}N/{x}[i]{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\#({x},M)\neq\mathsf{size}(C)\hskip 9.24994pt\widetilde{y}=(\mathsf{mlfv}(M)\!\setminus x)\uplus\mathsf{mlfv}(C)

\mathtt{[R:Fail^{\ell}]}

M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow{}\!\!\!\!\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(C)}\!\!\!\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}

\#({x},M)=\mathsf{size}(C)

U_{i}=\mathtt{1}^{!}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{head}(M)={x}[i]

\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}

M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\!\longrightarrow\!M{\{\!|}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}/{x}[i]{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{mlfv}(C)

\mathtt{[R:Cons_{1}]}

(\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}})\ C\star U\longrightarrow{}\!\!\!\!\!\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(C)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}\uplus\widetilde{y}}

\#(z,\widetilde{x})=\mathsf{size}(C)\ \widetilde{y}=\mathsf{mlfv}(C)

\mathtt{[R:Cons_{2}]}

\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow{}\!\!\!\!\!\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(C)}\mathtt{fail}^{(\widetilde{x}\setminus z)\uplus\widetilde{y}}

\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}

\mathtt{[R:ECont]}

D[\mathbb{M}]\longrightarrow D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]

M\longrightarrow\sum_{i=1}^{k}M^{\prime}_{i}

\mathtt{[R:TCont]}

C[M]\longrightarrow\sum_{i=1}^{k}C[M^{\prime}_{i}]

Figure 1:
Reduction rules for
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Reduction is defined by the rules in Fig.
1
.
Rule
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
induces explicit substitutions.
Resource consumption is implemented by two fetch rules, which open up explicit substitutions:

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{\ell}]}
, the
linear fetch
, ensures that the number of required resources matches the size of the linear bag
C
.
It induces a sum of terms with head substitutions, each denoting the partial evaluation of an element from
C
.
Thus, the size of
C
determines the summands in the resulting expression.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{!}]}
, the
unrestricted fetch
, consumes a resource occurring in a specific position of the unrestricted bag
U
via a linear head substitution of an unrestricted variable occurring in the head of the term. In this case, reduction results in an explicit substitution with
U
kept unaltered. Note that we check for the size of the linear bag
C
: in the case
\#(x,M)\neq\mathsf{size}(C)
, the term evolves to a linear failure via Rule
\mathtt{[R:\mathtt{fail}^{\ell}]}
(see Example
10
). This is another design choice: linear failure is prioritised in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Four rules show reduction to failure terms, and accumulate free variables involved in failed reductions.
Rules
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{\ell}]}
and
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
formalise the failure to evaluate an explicit substitution
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
.
The former rule targets a linear failure, which occurs when the size of
C
does not match the number of occurrences of
{x}
. The multiset
\widetilde{y}
preserves all free linear variables in
M
and
C
.
The latter rule targets an
unrestricted failure
, which occurs when the head of the term is
x[i]
and
U_{i}
(i.e., the
i
-th element of
U
) is empty.
In this case, failure preserves the free linear variables in
M
and
C
excluding the head unrestricted occurrence
x[i]
which is replaced by
\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}
.

Rules
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{1}]}
and
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{2}]}
describe reductions that lazily consume the failure term, when a term has
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}
at its head position.
The former rule consumes bags attached to it whilst preserving all its free linear variables; the latter rule consumes explicit substitution attached to it whilst also preserving all its free linear variables. The side condition
\#(z,\widetilde{x})=\mathsf{size}(C)
is necessary in Rule
\mathtt{[R:Cons_{2}]}
to avoid a clash with the premise of Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{\ell}]}
.
Finally, Rules
\mathtt{[R:ECont]}
and
\mathtt{[R:TCont]}
state closure by the
C
and
D
contexts (cf. Def.
8
).

Notice that the left-hand sides of the reduction rules in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
do not interfere with each other.
As a result, reduction in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
satisfies a
diamond property
: for all
\mathbb{M}\in\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, if there exist
\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathbb{M}_{2}\in\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
such that
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}_{1}
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}_{2}
, then there exists
\mathbb{N}\in\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
such that
\mathbb{M}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}\longleftarrow\mathbb{M}_{2}

(see App.
A
).

Notation 2
.

As usual,

\longrightarrow^{*}
denotes the reflexive-transitive closure of
\longrightarrow
.
We write
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R]}}\mathbb{M}
to denote that
\mathtt{[R]}
is the last (non-contextual) rule used in the step from
\mathbb{N}
to
\mathbb{M}
.

Example 9

(Cont. Example
3
)
.

We illustrate different reductions for
\lambda x.x
and
\lambda x.x[i]
.

1.

M_{1}=(\lambda x.x)(\lbag N\rbag\star U)
concerns a linear variable
x
with an linear bag containing one element. This is similar to the usual meaning of applying an identity function to a term:

\begin{aligned} (\lambda x.x)(\lbag N\rbag\star U)&\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag N\rbag\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{\ell}]}}x{\{\!|}N/x{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle=N\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{1}\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle,\end{aligned}

with a “garbage collector” that collects unused unrestricted resources.

2.

M_{2}=(\lambda x.x)(\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag N_{2}\rbag\star U)
concerns the case in which a linear variable
x
has a single occurrence but the linear bag has size two. Term
M_{2}
reduces to a sum of failure terms:

\begin{aligned} (\lambda x.x)(\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag N_{2}\rbag\star U)&\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}x\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag N_{2}\rbag\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Fail^{\ell}]}}\sum_{\mathsf{PER}(C)}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}\end{aligned}

for
C=\lbag N_{1}\rbag\cdot\lbag N_{2}\rbag
and
\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{mlfv}(C)
.

3.

M_{3}=(\lambda x.x[1])(\lbag N\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})
represents an abstraction of an unrestricted variable, which aims to consume the first element of the unrestricted bag. Because this bag is empty,
M_{3}
reduces to failure:

\begin{aligned} (\lambda x.x[1])(\lbag N\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})&\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:Beta]}}x[1]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag N\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R:\mathtt{fail}^{\ell}]}}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}},\end{aligned}

for
\widetilde{y}=\mathsf{mlfv}(N)
. Notice that
0=\#(x,x[1])\neq\mathsf{size}(\lbag N\rbag)=1
, since there are no linear occurrences of
x
in
x[1]
.

Example 10
.

To illustrate the need to check ‘
\mathsf{size}(C)
’ in
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
, consider the term
x[1]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag M\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, which features both a mismatch of linear bags for the linear variables to be substituted and an empty unrestricted bag with the need for the first element to be substituted.
We check the size of the linear bag because we wish to prioritise the reduction of Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{\ell}]}
. Hence, in case of a mismatch of linear resources we wish not to perform a reduction via Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
.
This is a design choice: our semantics collapses linear failure at the earliest moment it arises.

Example 11

(Cont. Example
4
)
.

Self-applications of
\Delta_{1}
do not behave as an expected variation of a lazy reduction from
\Omega
. Both

\Delta_{1}(\lbag\Delta_{1}\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})
and
\Delta_{1}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{1}\rbag^{!})
reduce to failure since the number of linear occurrences of
x
does not match the number of resources in the linear bag:

\begin{aligned} \Delta_{1}(\lbag\Delta_{1}\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})\longrightarrow(x(\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}))\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag\Delta_{1}\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}.\end{aligned}

The term
\Delta_{4}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!})
also fails: the linear bag is empty and there is one linear occurrence of
x
in
\Delta_{4}
. Note that
\Delta_{4}(\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!})
reduces to another application of
\Delta_{4}
before failing:

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}(\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!})

\displaystyle=(\lambda x.(x[1](\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!})))(\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R{:}Beta]}}(x[1](\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}))\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathtt{[R{:}Fetch^{!}]}}(\Delta_{4}(\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}))\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\mathtt{fail}^{\emptyset}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag x\rbag\star\mathtt{1}^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag\star\lbag\Delta_{4}\rbag^{!}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

Differently from
[
17
]
, there are terms in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
that when applied to each other behave similarly to
\Omega
, namely
\Omega_{5,6}
,
\Omega_{6,5}
, and
\Omega_{7}
(Example
4
).

\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

•

An interesting behaviour of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

\Delta

blue

2

\begin{aligned} \Omega_{5,6}&=\Delta_{5}(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag\Delta_{5}\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag\Delta_{6}\rbag^{!})\\

).
