Later on we will show that this term is well-formed (see Example
16
) with respect to the intersection type system introduced in
\lx@sectionsign

3
.

3
Well-Formed Expressions via Intersection Types

We define
well-formed

\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-expressions by relying on a non-idempotent intersection type system, based on the system by Bucciarelli et al.
[
4
]
.
Our system for well-formed expressions subsumes the one in
[
17
]
: it uses
strict
and
multiset
types to check linear bags; moreover, it uses
list
and
tuple
types to check unrestricted bags.
As in
[
17
]
, we write
“well-formedness” (of terms, bags, and expressions) to stress that, unlike usual type systems, our system can account for terms that may reduce to the failure term (cf. Remark
18
).

Definition 13

(Types for
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

We define strict, multiset,
list
, and
tuple types
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{.8cm}}l}\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{.2cm}}rcl}\text{(Strict)}\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&\quad\sigma,\tau,\delta&::=&\mathbf{unit}\ |\ (\pi,\eta)\rightarrow\sigma\\
\text{(Multiset)}\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&\quad\pi,\zeta&::=&\bigwedge_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}\ |\ \omega\end{array}\hfil\hskip 22.76228pt&\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{.2cm}}rcl}\text{(List)}\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&\quad\eta,\epsilon&::=&\sigma\ |\ \epsilon\diamond\eta\\
\text{(Tuple)}\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&\quad(\pi,\eta)\end{array}\end{array}

A strict type can be the
\mathbf{unit}
type or a functional type
(\pi,\eta)\rightarrow\sigma
, where

(\pi,\eta)
is a tuple type and
\sigma
is a strict type. Multiset types can be
either the empty type
\omega
or
an intersection of strict types
\bigwedge_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}
, with
I
non-empty. The operator
\wedge
is commutative, associative, non-idempotent, that is,
\sigma\wedge\sigma\neq\sigma
, with identity
\omega
. The intersection type
\bigwedge_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}
is the type of a linear bag; the cardinality of
I
corresponds to its size.

A list type can be either an strict type
\sigma
or the composition
\epsilon\diamond\eta
of two list types
\epsilon
and
\eta
. We use the list type
\epsilon\diamond\eta
to type the concatenation of two unrestricted bags.
A tuple type
(\pi,\eta)
types the concatenation of a linear bag of type
\pi
with an unrestricted bag of type
\eta
.
Notice that a list type
\epsilon\diamond\eta
can be recursively unfolded into a finite composition of strict types
\sigma_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\sigma_{n}
, for some
n\geq 1
. In this case the length of
\epsilon\diamond\eta
is
n
and that
\sigma_{i}
is its
i
-th strict type, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Notation 3
.

Given
k\geq 0
, we write
\sigma^{k}
to stand for
\sigma\wedge\cdots\wedge\sigma

(
k
times, if
k>0
) or for
\omega
(if
k=0
).
Similarly,
\hat{x}:\sigma^{k}

stands for
x:\sigma,\cdots,x:\sigma
(
k
times, if
k>0
)
or for
x:\omega
(if
k=0
). Given
k\geq 1
, we write
x^{!}:\eta
to stand for
x[1]:\eta_{1},\cdots,x[k]:\eta_{k}
.

Notation 4

(
\eta\varpropto\epsilon
)
.

Let
\epsilon
and
\eta
be two list types, with the length of
\epsilon
greater or equal to that of
\eta
.
Let us write
\epsilon_{i}
and
\eta_{i}
to denote the
i
-th strict type in
\epsilon
and
\eta
, respectively.
We write
\eta\varpropto\epsilon
meaning the initial sublist, whenever there exist
\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
such that: i)
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}\diamond\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
; ii) the size of
\epsilon^{\prime}
is that of
\eta
; iii) for all
i
,
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}=\eta_{i}
.

Linear contexts
range over
\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
and
unrestricted contexts
range over
\Theta,\Upsilon,\ldots
. They are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{.9cm}}c}\Gamma,\Delta::=\text{-}~|~x:\sigma~\mid~\Gamma,x:\sigma\hfil\hskip 25.6073pt&\Theta,\Upsilon::=\text{-}~|~x^{!}:\eta~|~\Theta,x^{!}:\eta\end{array}

The empty linear/unrestricted type assignment is denoted ‘
-
’.
Linear variables can occur more than once in a linear context; they are assigned only strict types.
For instance,
x:(\tau,\sigma)\rightarrow\tau,x:\tau
is a valid context: it means that
x
can be of both type
(\tau,\sigma)\rightarrow\tau
and
\tau
.
In contrast, unrestricted variables can occur at most once in unrestricted contexts; they are assigned only list types.
The multiset of linear variables in
\Gamma
is denoted as
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
; similarly,
\mathtt{dom}(\Theta)
denotes the set of unrestricted variables in
\Theta
.

Judgements are of the form
\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma
, where the left-hand side contexts are separated by “;” and
\mathbb{M}:\sigma
means that
\mathbb{M}
has type
\sigma
. We write
\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma
to denote
\text{-}\,;\,\text{-}\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma
.

\mathtt{[F{:}var^{\ell}]}

\Theta;{x}:\sigma\models{x}:\sigma

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;{x}:\eta_{i},\Delta\models{x}:\sigma

\mathtt{[F{:}var^{!}]}

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Delta\models{x}[i]:\sigma

\mathtt{[F{:}\mathtt{1}^{\ell}]}

\Theta;\text{-}\models\mathtt{1}:\omega

\mathtt{[F{:}\mathtt{1}^{!}]}

\Theta;\text{-}\models\mathtt{1}^{!}:\sigma

\Theta,z^{!}:\eta;\Gamma,{\hat{z}}:\sigma^{k}\models M:\tau\hskip 9.24994ptz\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\mathtt{[F{:}abs]}

\Theta;\Gamma\models\lambda z.M:(\sigma^{k},\eta)\rightarrow\tau

\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta;\Gamma\models M:(\sigma^{j},\eta)\rightarrow\tau

\Theta;\Delta\models B:(\sigma^{k},\epsilon)

\eta\varpropto\epsilon

\mathtt{[F{:}app]}

\Theta;\Gamma,\Delta\models M\ B:\tau

~\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Gamma,{\hat{x}}:\sigma^{j}\models M:\tau

\Theta;\Delta\models B:(\sigma^{k},\epsilon)

\eta\varpropto\epsilon

\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}

\Theta;\Gamma,\Delta\models M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

\Theta;\Gamma\models C:\sigma^{k}

\Theta;\text{-}\models U:\eta

\mathtt{[F{:}bag]}

\Theta;\Gamma\models C\star U:(\sigma^{k},\eta)

\Theta;\Gamma\models M:\sigma

\Theta;\Delta\models C:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[F{:}bag^{\ell}]}

\Theta;\Gamma,\Delta\models\lbag M\rbag\cdot C:\sigma^{k+1}

\Theta;\text{-}\models M:\sigma

\mathtt{[F{:}bag^{!}]}

\Theta;\text{-}\models\lbag M\rbag^{!}:\sigma

\Theta;\text{-}\models U:\epsilon

\Theta;\text{-}\models V:\eta

\mathtt{[F{:}\diamond bag^{!}]}

\Theta;\text{-}\models U\diamond V:\epsilon\diamond\eta

\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)=\widetilde{x}

\mathtt{[F{:}fail]}

\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}:\tau

\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\sigma\hskip 18.49988pt\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\mathtt{[F{:}sum]}

\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}:\sigma

\Theta;\Gamma\models M:\sigma\hskip 9.24994ptx\not\in\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\mathtt{[F{:}weak]}

\Theta;\Gamma,x:\omega\models M:\sigma

Figure 3:
Well-formedness rules for
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(cf. Def.
14
). In Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}app]}
and
\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
:
k,j\geq 0
.

Definition 14

(Well-formed
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expressions)
.

An expression
\mathbb{M}
is well-formed (wf, for short) if there exist
\Gamma
,
\Theta
and
\tau
such that
\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
is entailed via the rules in Fig.
3
.

We describe the well-formedness rules in Fig.
3
.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F:var^{\ell}]}
and
\mathtt{[F:var^{!}]}
assign types to linear and unrestricted variables, respectively.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[F:var^{!}]}
resembles the
copy
rule
[
6
]
where we use a linear copy of an unrestricted variable
x[i]
of type
\sigma
, typed with
x^{!}:\eta
, and type the linear copy with the corresponding strict type
\eta_{i}
which in this case the linear copy
x
would have type equal to
\sigma
.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F:\mathtt{1}^{\ell}]}
and
\mathtt{[F:\mathtt{1}^{!}]}
assign types to the empty linear/unrestricted bag:
\mathtt{1}
has type
\omega
, whereas
\mathtt{1}^{!}
has an arbitrary strict type
\sigma
. Arbitrariness is allowed since the substitution of an unrestricted variable for
1^{!}
leads to a
\mathtt{fail}
term (Rule
\mathtt{[R:Fail^{!}]}
), which has an arbitrary strict type.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[F:abs]}
assigns type
(\sigma^{k},\eta)\to\tau
to an abstraction
\lambda z.M
, provided that the unrestricted occurrences of
z
may be typed by the unrestricted context containing
z^{!}:\eta
, the linear occurrences of
z
are typed with the linear context containing
\hat{z}:\sigma^{k}
, for some
k\geq 0
, and there are no other linear occurrences of
z
in the linear context
\Gamma
.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}app]}
and
\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
(for application and explicit substitution, resp.) use the condition
\eta\varpropto\epsilon
(cf. Notation
4
), which captures the portion of the unrestricted bag that is effectively used in a term: it
ensures that
\epsilon
can be decomposed into some
\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
, such that each type component
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}
matches with
\eta_{i}
. If this requirement is satisfied, Rule
\mathtt{[F{:}app]}
types an application
M\ B
given that
M
has a functional type in which the left of the arrow is a tuple type
(\sigma^{j},\eta)
whereas the bag
B
is typed with tuple
(\sigma^{k},\epsilon)
. Similarly, Rule
\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
types the term
M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
provided that
B
has the tuple type
(\sigma^{k},\epsilon)
and
M
is typed with the variable
x
having linear type assignment
\sigma^{j}
and unrestricted type assignment
\eta
.

Remark 15
.

Differently from intersection type systems
[
4
,
16
]
, in Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}app]}
and
\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
there is no equality requirement between
j
and
k
, as we would like to capture terms that fail due to a mismatch in resources: we only require that the linear part of the tuples are composed of the same strict type, say
\sigma
.
As a term can take an unrestricted bag with arbitrary size we only require that the elements of the unrestricted bag that are used have a “consistent” type, i.e., the type of the unrestricted bag satisfies the relation
\varpropto
with the unrestricted fragment of the corresponding tuple type.

There are four rules for bags:

•

Rule
\mathtt{[F:bag^{!}]}
types an unrestricted bag
\lbag M\rbag^{!}
with the type
\sigma
of
M
. Note that
\lbag x\rbag^{!}
, an unrestricted bag containing a linear variable
x
, is not well-formed, whereas
\lbag x[i]\rbag^{!}
is well-formed.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[F:bag]}
assigns the tuple type
(\sigma^{k},\eta)
to the concatenation of a linear bag of type
\sigma^{k}
with an unrestricted bag of type
\eta
.

•

Rules
\mathtt{[F:bag^{\ell}]}

and
\mathtt{[F{:}\diamond bag^{!}]}
type the concatenation of linear and unrestricted bags.

•

Rule
\mathtt{[F{:}\mathtt{1}^{!}]}
allows an empty unrestricted bag to have an arbitrary
\sigma
type since it may be referred to by a variable for substitution: we must be able to compare its type with the type of unrestricted variables that may consume the empty bag (this reduction would inevitably lead to failure).

As in
[
17
]
, Rule
\mathtt{[F{:}\mathtt{fail}]}
handles the failure term, and is the main difference with respect to standard type systems. Rules for sums and weakening (
\mathtt{[F:sum]}
and
\mathtt{[F:weak]}
) are standard.

Example 16

(Cont. Example
12
)
.

Term
\Delta_{7}:=\lambda x.x[2](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!})
is well-formed, as ensured by the judgement
\Theta;\text{-}\models\Delta_{7}:(\omega,\sigma^{\prime}\diamond(\sigma^{j},\sigma^{\prime}\diamond\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau)\rightarrow\tau
, whose derivation is given below:

•

\Pi_{3}
is the derivation of

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\text{-}\models\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}:\sigma^{\prime},
for
\eta=\sigma^{\prime}\diamond(\sigma^{j},\sigma^{\prime}\diamond\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau
.

•

\Pi_{4}
is the derivation:

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\text{-}\models{x}[2]:(\sigma^{j},\sigma^{\prime}\diamond\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau

•

\Pi_{5}
is the derivation:
\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;x:\omega\models(\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}):(\omega,\sigma^{\prime}\diamond\sigma^{\prime})

Therefore,

\Pi_{5}

\Pi_{4}

\sigma^{\prime}\diamond\sigma^{\prime}\varpropto\sigma^{\prime}\diamond\sigma^{\prime}

\mathtt{[F{:}app]}

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;x:\omega\models x[2](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}):\tau

\mathtt{[F{:}abs]}

\Theta;\text{-}\models\underbrace{\lambda x.(x[2](\mathtt{1}\star\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}\diamond\lbag x[1]\rbag^{!}))}_{\Delta_{7}}:(\omega,\eta)\rightarrow\tau

Well-formed expressions satisfy subject reduction (SR);
see App.
B
for a proof.

Theorem 17

(SR in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

If
\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau
and
\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
then
\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}^{\prime}:\tau
.

Proof.

By structural induction on the reduction rules.
We proceed by analysing the rule applied in
\mathbb{M}
.
An interesting case occurs when the rule is
\mathtt{[F:Fetch^{!}]}
: Then
\mathbb{M}=M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle
, where
U=\lbag N_{1}\rbag^{!}\diamond\cdots\diamond\lbag N_{l}\rbag^{!}
and
\mathsf{head}(M)={x}[i]
. The reduction is as follows:

\mathsf{head}(M)={x}[i]

U_{i}=\lbag N_{i}\rbag^{!}

\mathtt{[R:Fetch^{!}]}

M\ \langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle\longrightarrow M{\{\!|}N_{i}/{x}[i]{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

By hypothesis, one has the derivation:

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Gamma^{\prime},\hat{x}:\sigma^{j}\models M:\tau

\Pi

\Theta;\cdot\models U:\epsilon

\Theta;\Delta\models{C}:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[F{:}bag]}

\Theta;\Delta\models C\star U:(\sigma^{k},\epsilon)

\eta\varpropto\epsilon

\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}

\Theta;\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta\models M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

where
\Pi
has the form

\Theta;\cdot\models N_{1}:\epsilon_{1}

\mathtt{[F{:}bag^{!}]}

\Theta;\cdot\models\lbag N_{1}\rbag^{!}:\epsilon_{1}

\cdots

\Theta;\cdot\models N_{l}:\epsilon_{l}

\mathtt{[F{:}bag^{!}]}

\Theta;\cdot\models\lbag N_{l}\rbag^{!}:\epsilon_{l}

\mathtt{[F{:}\diamond bag^{!}]}

\Theta;\cdot\models\lbag N_{1}\rbag^{!}\diamond\cdots\diamond\lbag N_{l}\rbag^{!}:\epsilon

with
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta
. Notice that if
\epsilon_{i}=\delta
and
\eta\varpropto\epsilon
then
\eta_{i}=\delta
.
By Lemma
35
, there
exists a derivation

\Pi_{1}
of
\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Gamma^{\prime},\hat{x}:\sigma^{j}\models M{\{\!|}N_{i}/{x}[i]{|\!\}}:\tau
. Therefore, we have:

\Theta,x^{!}:\eta;\Gamma^{\prime},\hat{x}:\sigma^{j}\models M{\{\!|}N_{1}/{x}[i]{|\!\}}:\tau

\Theta;\cdot\models U:\epsilon

\Theta;\Delta\models{C}:\sigma^{k}

\mathtt{[F{:}bag]}

\Theta;\Delta\models C\star U:(\sigma^{k},\epsilon)

\eta\varpropto\epsilon

\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}

\Theta;\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta\models M{\{\!|}N_{i}/{x[i]}{|\!\}}\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle:\tau

∎

Remark 18

(Well-Formed vs Well-Typed Expressions)
.

Our type system (and Theorem
17
) can be specialised to the case of
well-typed
expressions that do not contain (and never reduce to) the failure term.
In particular, Rules
\mathtt{[F{:}app]}
and
\mathtt{[F{:}ex\text{-}sub]}
would need to check that
\sigma^{k}=\sigma^{j}
, as failure can be caused due to a mismatch of linear resources. A difference between well typed and well formed expressions is that the former satisfy subject expansion, but the latter do not: expressions that lead to failure can be ill-typed yet failure itself is well-formed.

4
A Typed Encoding of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into Concurrent Processes

We encode
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
,
a session
\pi
-calculus that stands on a Curry-Howard correspondence between linear logic and session types (
\lx@sectionsign

4.1
). We
extend the two-step approach that we devised in
[
17
]
for the sub-calculus
\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(with linear resources only) (cf. Fig.
4
).
First, in
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
, we define an encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
from well-formed expressions in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to well-formed expressions in a variant of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
with
sharing
, dubbed
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
(
\lx@sectionsign

4.2
).
Then, in
\lx@sectionsign

4.4
, we define an encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
(for a name
u
) from well-formed expressions in
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to well-typed processes in
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

We prove that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
satisfy well-established correctness criteria
[
9
,
14
]
:
type preservation
,
operational completeness
,
operational soundness
, and
success sensitiveness

(cf. App.
E.1
).
Because
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
includes unrestricted resources, the results given here strictly generalise those in
[
17
]
.

4.1
\mathsf{s}\pi
: A Session-Typed
\pi
-Calculus

\mathsf{s}\pi
is a
\pi
-calculus with
session types

[
11
,
12
]
, which ensure that the endpoints of a channel perform matching actions.
We consider the full process framework in
[
5
]
, including constructs for specifying labelled choices and client/server connections; they will be useful to codify unrestricted resources and variables in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
Following
[
6
,
18
]
,

\mathsf{s}\pi
stands on a Curry-Howard correspondence between session types and a linear logic with dual modalities/types (
{\binampersand}A
and
\oplus A
), which define
non-deterministic
session behaviour.
As in
[
6
,
18
]
,
in
\mathsf{s}\pi
, cut elimination corresponds to communication, proofs to processes, and propositions to session types.

\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
\mathsf{s}\pi
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
\lx@sectionsign

4.4
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}

Figure 4:
Our two-step approach to encode
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

Syntax.

Names

x,y,z,w\ldots
denote the endpoints of protocols specified by session types. We write
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
for the capture-avoiding substitution of
x
for
y
in process
P
.

Definition 19

(Processes)
.

The syntax of
\mathsf{s}\pi
processes is given by the grammar below.

\displaystyle P,Q::=~

\displaystyle{\bf 0}\ |\ \overline{x}(y).P\ |\ x(y).P\ |\ {x}.\mathtt{l}_{i};P\ |\ {x}.\mathtt{case}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:P_{i}\}\ |\ x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\ |\ x.\mathtt{close};P

\displaystyle\ |\

\displaystyle(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q)\ |\ [x\leftrightarrow y]\ |\ (\nu x)P\ |\ ~!x(y).P\ |\ \overline{x}?(y).P

\displaystyle\ |\

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\ |\ x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\ |\ x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};P\ |\ (P\oplus Q)

Process
{\bf 0}
denotes inaction.
Process
\overline{x}(y).P
sends a fresh name
y
along
x
and then continues as
P
. Process

x(y).P
receives a name
z
along
x
and then continues as
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
. Process
{x}.\mathtt{case}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:P_{i}\}
is a branching construct, with labelled alternatives indexed by the finite set
I
: it awaits a choice on
x
with continuation
P_{j}
for each
j\in I
. Process
{x}.\mathtt{l}_{i};P
selects on
x
the alternative indexed by
i
before continuing as
P
. Processes
x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}
and
x.\mathtt{close};P
are complementary actions for closing session
x
.
We sometimes use the shorthand notations
\overline{y}[\,]
and
y[\,];P
to stand for
{y}.\overline{\mathtt{close}}
and
y.\mathtt{close};P
, respectively.
Process
P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q
is the parallel execution of
P
and
Q
.
The forwarder process
[x\leftrightarrow y]
denotes a bi-directional link between sessions
x
and
y
.
Process
(\nu x)P
denotes the process
P
in which name
x
is kept private (local) to
P
.
Process
!x(y).P
defines a server that spawns copies of
P
upon requests on
x
.
Process
\overline{x}?(y).P
denotes a client that connects to a server by sending the fresh name
y
on
x
.

The remaining constructs come from
[
5
]
and introduce non-deterministic sessions which
may
provide a session protocol
or
fail.
Process
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P
confirms that the session on
x
will execute and continues as
P
. Process
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
signals the failure of implementing the session on
x
.
Process
x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};P
specifies a dependency on a non-deterministic session
x
.
This process can either (i) synchronise with an action
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
and continue as
P
, or (ii) synchronise with an action
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
, discard
P
, and propagate the failure on
x
to
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})
, which are sessions implemented in
P
.
When
x
is the only session implemented in
P
, there is no tuple of dependencies
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})
and so we write simply
x.\mathtt{some};P
.
Finally, process
P\oplus Q
denotes a non-deterministic choice between
P
and
Q
. We shall often write
\bigoplus_{i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}}P_{i}
to stand for
P_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus P_{n}
.
In
(\nu y)P
and
x(y).P
the occurrence of name
y
is binding, with scope
P
.
The set of free names of
P
is denoted by
\mathit{fn}(P)
.

Semantics.

The
reduction relation
of
\mathsf{s}\pi
specifies the computations that a process performs on its own (cf. Fig.
5
).
It is closed by
structural congruence
, denoted
\equiv
, which expresses basic identities for processes and the non-collapsing nature of non-determinism
(cf. App.
C
).

\displaystyle\overline{x}{(y)}.Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x(y).P\longrightarrow(\nu y)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};Q\longrightarrow P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q

\displaystyle Q\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\Rightarrow P\oplus Q\longrightarrow P\oplus Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{close};P\longrightarrow P

\displaystyle{x}.\mathtt{l}_{i};Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{x}.\mathtt{case}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:P_{i}\}\longrightarrow Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P_{i}

\displaystyle!x(y).Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\overline{x}?(y).P\longrightarrow(\nu x)(!x(y).Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}(\nu y)(Q\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P))

\displaystyle(\nu x)([x\leftrightarrow y]\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}P)\longrightarrow P\{\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$y$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\hskip 9.24994pt(x\neq y)

\displaystyle P\equiv P^{\prime}\wedge P^{\prime}\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\wedge Q^{\prime}\equiv Q\Rightarrow P\longrightarrow Q

\displaystyle Q\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\Rightarrow P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q\longrightarrow P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow Q\Rightarrow(\nu y)P\longrightarrow(\nu y)Q

x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})};{Q}\longrightarrow w_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\cdots\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}w_{n}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}

Figure 5:
Reduction for
\mathsf{s}\pi

The first reduction rule formalises communication, which concerns bound names only (internal mobility), as
y
is bound in
\overline{x}{(y)}.Q
and
x(y).P
.
Reduction for the forwarder process leads to a substitution.
The reduction rule for closing a session is self-explanatory, as is the rule in which prefix
x.\overline{\mathtt{some}}
confirms the availability of a non-deterministic session. When a non-deterministic session is not available,
x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
triggers this failure to all dependent sessions
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
; this may in turn trigger further failures (i.e., on sessions that depend on
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
).
The remaining rules define contextual reduction with respect to restriction, composition, and non-deterministic choice.

Type System

Session types govern the behaviour of the names of a process.
An assignment
x:A
enforces the use of name
x
according to the protocol specified by
A
.

Definition 20

(Session Types)
.

Session types are given by

\begin{array}[]{rl}A,B::=&\bot\ |\ \mathbf{1}\ |\ A\otimes B\ |\ A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\ |\ \oplus_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\}\ |\ {\binampersand}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\}\ |\ !A\ |\ ?A\ |\ {\binampersand}A\ |\ \oplus A\end{array}

The multiplicative units
\bot
and
\mathbf{1}
are used to type closed session endpoints.
We use
A\otimes B
to type a name that first outputs a name of type
A
before proceeding as specified by
B
.
Similarly,
A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B
types a name that first inputs a name of type
A
before proceeding as specified by
B
.
Then,
!A
types a name that repeatedly provides a service specified by
A
.
Dually,
?A
is the type of a name that can connect to a server offering
A
.
Types

\oplus_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\}
and
{\binampersand}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\}

are assigned to names that can select and offer a labelled choice, respectively.
Then we have the two modalities introduced in
[
5
]
.
We use
{\binampersand}A
as the type of a (non-deterministic) session that
may produce
a behaviour of type
A
.
Dually,
\oplus A
denotes the type of a session that
may consume
a behaviour of type
A
.

The two endpoints of a session should be
dual
to ensure absence of communication errors. The dual of a type
A
is denoted
\overline{A}
.
Duality corresponds to negation
(\cdot)^{\bot}
in linear logic
[
5
]
.

Definition 21

(Duality)
.

Duality on types is given by:

\small\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c}\overline{\mathbf{1}}=\bot\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{\bot}=\mathbf{1}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{A\otimes B}=\overline{A}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\overline{B}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{\oplus_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\}}={\binampersand}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:\overline{A_{i}}\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{\oplus A}={\binampersand}\overline{A}\\
\overline{!A}=?A\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{?A}=!A\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B}=\overline{A}\otimes\overline{B}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{{\binampersand}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\}}=\oplus_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:\overline{A_{i}}\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\overline{{\binampersand}A}=\oplus\overline{A}\end{array}\end{array}

Judgements are of the form
P\vdash\Delta;\Theta
, where
P
is a process,
\Delta
is the linear context, and
\Theta
is the unrestricted context.
Both
\Delta
and
\Theta
contain assignments of types to names, but satisfy different substructural principles: while
\Theta
satisfies weakening, contraction and exchange,
\Delta
only satisfies exchange. The empty context is denoted ‘
\cdot
’.
We write
{\binampersand}\Delta
to denote that all assignments in
\Delta
have a non-deterministic type, i.e.,
\Delta=w_{1}{:}{\binampersand}A_{1},\ldots,w_{n}{:}{\binampersand}A_{n}
, for some
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
.
The typing judgement
P\vdash\Delta
corresponds to the logical sequent for classical linear logic, which can be recovered by erasing processes and name assignments.

Typing rules for processes in Fig.
6
correspond to proof rules in linear logic;
we discuss some of them.
Rule
\mathtt{[Tid]}
interprets the identity axiom using the forwarder process.
Rules
\mathtt{[T\mathbf{1}]}
and
\mathtt{[T\bot]}
type the process constructs for session termination.
Rules
\mathtt{[T\otimes]}
and
\mathtt{[T\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}]}
type output and input of a name along a session, resp.
The last four rules are used to type process constructs related to non-determinism and failure.
Rules
\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}_{d}^{x}]}
and
\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}^{x}]}
introduce a session of type
{\binampersand}A
, which may produce a behaviour of type
A
: while the former rule covers the case in which
x:A
is indeed available, the latter rule formalises the case in which
x:A
is not available (i.e., a failure).
Given a sequence of names
\widetilde{w}=w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
, Rule
\mathtt{[T\oplus^{x}_{\widetilde{w}}]}
accounts for the possibility of not being able
to consume the session
x:A
by considering sessions different from
x
as potentially not available.
Rule
\mathtt{[T\oplus]}
expresses non-deterministic choice of processes
P
and
Q
that implement non-deterministic behaviours only.
Finally, Rule
\mathtt{[T\oplus_{i}]}
and
\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}]}
correspond, resp., to selection and branching: the former provides a selection of behaviours along
x
as long as
P
is guarded with the
i
-th behaviour; the latter offers a labelled choice where each behaviour
A_{i}
is matched to a corresponding
P_{i}
.

\mathtt{[Tid]}

[x\leftrightarrow y]\vdash x{:}A,y{:}\overline{A};\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T\mathbf{1}]}

x.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\vdash x:\mathbf{1};\,\Theta

P\vdash\Delta;\Theta

\mathtt{[T\bot]}

x.\mathtt{close};P\vdash x{:}\bot,\Delta;\,\Theta

P\vdash\Delta,y:{A};\,\Theta\hskip 9.24994ptQ\vdash\Delta^{\prime},x:B;\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T\otimes]}

\overline{x}(y).(P\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q)\vdash\Delta,\Delta^{\prime},x:A\otimes B;\,\Theta

P\vdash\Delta,y:C,x:D;\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}]}

x(y).P\vdash\Delta,x:C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D;\,\Theta

P\;{\vdash}\widetilde{w}:{\binampersand}\Delta,x:A;\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T\oplus^{x}_{\widetilde{w}}]}

x.\mathtt{some}_{\widetilde{w}};P\vdash\widetilde{w}{:}{\binampersand}\Delta,x{:}\oplus A;\,\Theta

P\vdash\Delta,x:A;\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}_{d}^{x}]}

x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};P\vdash\Delta,x:{\binampersand}A;\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}^{x}]}

x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\vdash x:{\binampersand}A;\,\Theta

P\vdash{\binampersand}\Delta;\,\Theta\hskip 18.49988ptQ\;{\vdash}{\binampersand}\Delta;\,\Theta

\mathtt{[T\oplus]}

P\oplus Q\vdash{\binampersand}\Delta;\,\Theta

P\vdash\Delta,x:A_{i};\Theta

\mathtt{[T\oplus_{i}]}

{x}.\mathtt{l}_{i};P\vdash\Delta,x:\oplus_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\};\Theta

P_{i}\vdash\Delta,x:A_{i};\Theta\hskip 9.24994pt(\forall i\in I)

\mathtt{[T{\binampersand}]}

{x}.\mathtt{case}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:P_{i}\}\vdash\Delta,x:{\binampersand}_{i\in I}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:A_{i}\};\Theta

P\vdash\Delta;x:A,\Theta

\mathtt{[T?]}

P\vdash\Delta,x:?A;\Theta

P\vdash y:A;\Theta

\mathtt{[T!]}

!x(y).P\vdash x:!A;\Theta

P\;{\vdash}\Delta,y:A;x:A,\Theta

\mathtt{[Tcopy]}

\overline{x}?(y).P\vdash\Delta;x:A,\Theta

Figure 6:
Typing rules for
\mathsf{s}\pi
.

The type system enjoys type preservation, a result that
follows from the cut elimination property in linear logic; it ensures that the observable interface of a system is invariant under reduction.
The type system also ensures other properties for well-typed processes (e.g. global progress, strong normalisation, and confluence); see
[
5
]
for details.

Theorem 22

(Type Preservation
[
5
]
)
.

If
P\vdash\Delta;\,\Theta
and
P\longrightarrow Q
then
Q\vdash\Delta;\,\Theta
.

4.2
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
: An Auxiliary Calculus With Sharing

To facilitate the encoding of
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, we define
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
: an auxiliary calculus whose constructs are inspired by the work of Gundersen et al.
[
10
]
, Ghilezan et al.
[
8
]
, and Kesner and Lengrand
[
13
]
. The syntax of
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
only modifies the syntax of terms, which is defined by the grammar below; variables
{x}[*]
, bags
B
, and expressions
\mathbb{M}
are as in
\Cref
def:rsyntaxfailunres.

\displaystyle\mbox{(Terms)}\quad M,N,L::=

\displaystyle~~{x}[*]\ |\ \lambda x.(M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}])\ |\ (M\ B)\ |\ M{\langle\!|}N/{x}{|\!\rangle}\ |\ M\llfloor U/x\rrceil

\displaystyle\ |\

\displaystyle~~\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\ |\ M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]\ |\ (M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}])\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle

We consider the
sharing construct

M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
and two kinds of explicit substitutions: the
explicit linear substitution
, written
M{\langle\!|}N/{x}{|\!\rangle}
, and the
explicit unrestricted substitution
, written
M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil
.
The term
M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]
defines the sharing of variables
{\widetilde{x}}
occurring in
M
using the linear variable
{x}
.
We shall refer to
{x}
as
sharing variable
and to
{\widetilde{x}}
as
shared variables
. A linear variable is only allowed to appear once in a term.
Notice that
{\widetilde{x}}
can be empty:
M[\leftarrow{x}]
expresses that
{x}
does not share any variables in
M
.
As in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, the term
\mathtt{fail}^{{\widetilde{x}}}
explicitly accounts for failed attempts at substituting the variables in
{\widetilde{x}}
.

We summarise some requirements.
In
M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
, we require: (i) every
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
occurs exactly once in
M
and that (ii)
x_{i}
is not a sharing variable.
The occurrence of
x_{i}
can appear within the fail term
\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{y}}
, if
x_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
.
In the explicit linear substitution
M{\langle\!|}N/{x}{|\!\rangle}
, we require: the variable
x
has to occur in
M
;
x
cannot be a sharing variable; and
x
cannot be in an explicit linear substitution occurring in
M
; all free
linear
occurrences of
x
in
M
are bound. In the explicit unrestricted substitution
M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil
, we require: all free
unrestricted
occurrences of
x
in
M
are bound;
{x}
cannot be in an explicit unrestricted substitution occurring in
M
.
This way, e.g.,
M^{\prime}{\langle\!|}L/{x}{|\!\rangle}{\langle\!|}N/{x}{|\!\rangle}

and
M^{\prime}{\langle\!|}U^{\prime}/{x}{|\!\rangle}{\langle\!|}U/{x}{|\!\rangle}

are not valid terms in
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

The following congruence will be important when proving encoding correctness.

Definition 23
.

The congruence
\equiv_{\lambda}
for
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
on terms and expressions is given by the identities below.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil&\equiv_{\lambda}&M,\ x\not\in M\\
(MB){\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}&\equiv_{\lambda}&(M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle})B,\ x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
(MB)\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil&\equiv_{\lambda}&(M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil)B,\ x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\\
(MA)[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}&(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)A,\ x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}\Rightarrow x_{i}\not\in\mathsf{fv}(A)\\
M[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$A$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle&\equiv_{\lambda}&(M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$B$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle)[\widetilde{y}\leftarrow y]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$A$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\rangle\!\rangle,\ \begin{aligned} x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}\Rightarrow x_{i}\not\in\mathsf{fv}(A),\\
y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}\Rightarrow y_{i}\not\in\mathsf{fv}(B)\end{aligned}\\[2.84526pt]
M{\langle\!|}N_{2}/y{|\!\rangle}{\langle\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\rangle}&\equiv_{\lambda}&M{\langle\!|}N_{1}/x{|\!\rangle}{\langle\!|}N_{2}/y{|\!\rangle},x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(N_{2}),y\notin\mathsf{fv}(N_{1})\\[2.84526pt]
M\llfloor U_{2}/{y}\rrceil\llfloor U_{1}/{x}\rrceil&\equiv_{\lambda}&M\llfloor U_{1}/{x}\rrceil\llfloor U_{2}/{y}\rrceil,x\not\in\mathsf{fv}(U_{2}),y\notin\mathsf{fv}(U_{1})\\[2.84526pt]
C[M]&\equiv_{\lambda}&C[M^{\prime}],\ \text{with }M\equiv_{\lambda}M^{\prime}\\
D[\mathbb{M}]&\equiv_{\lambda}&D[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}],\ \text{with }\mathbb{M}\equiv_{\lambda}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\end{array}

The first rule states that we may remove unneeded unrestricted substitutions when the variable in concern does not appear within the term. The next three identities enforce that bags can always be moved in and out of all forms of explicit substitution, which are useful manipulate expressions and to form a redex for Rule
\mathtt{[R:Beta]}
. The other rules deal with permutation of explicit substitutions and contextual closure.

Well-formedness for
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
, based on intersection types, is defined as in
\lx@sectionsign

3
;
see App.
D
.

4.3
Encoding
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

We define an encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
from well-formed terms in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
. This encoding relies on an intermediate encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
on
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms.

Notation 5
.

Given a term
M
such that
\#(x,M)=k

and a sequence of pairwise distinct fresh variables
\widetilde{x}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
we write
M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle
or
M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle
to stand for

M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\cdots\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{k}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle
, i.e., a simultaneous linear substitution whereby each distinct linear occurrence of
x
in
M
is replaced by a distinct
x_{i}\in\widetilde{x}
.
Notice that each
x_{i}
has the same type as
x
.
We use (simultaneous) linear substitutions to force all bound linear variables in
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to become shared variables in
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.

Definition 24

(From
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
to
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)
.

Let
M\in\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
Suppose
\Theta;\Gamma\models{M}:\tau
, with

\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)=\mathsf{lfv}(M)=\{{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{k}\}
and
\#({x}_{i},M)=j_{i}
.
We define
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
as

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\widetilde{x}_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small${x}_{1}$}\rangle\cdots\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small${\widetilde{x}_{k}}$}\!/\mbox{\small${x}_{k}$}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[{\widetilde{x}_{1}}\leftarrow{x}_{1}]\cdots[{\widetilde{x}_{k}}\leftarrow{x}_{k}]

where
{\widetilde{x}_{i}}={x}_{i_{1}},\cdots,{x}_{{j_{i}}}

and the encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}:\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\to u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
is defined in Fig.
7
on
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms.
The encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
extends homomorphically to expressions.

The encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
converts
n
occurrences of
x
in a term into
n
distinct variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
The sharing construct coordinates them by constraining each to occur exactly once within a term.
We proceed in two stages.
First, we share all linear free linear variables using
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
: this ensures that free variables are replaced by shared variables which are then bound by the sharing construct.
Second, we apply the encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
on the corresponding term.
The encoding is presented in Fig.
7
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
maintains
x[i]
unaltered, and acts homomorphically over concatenation of bags and explicit substitutions. The encoding renames bound variables with bound shared variables.
As we will see, this will enable a tight operational correspondence result with
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
In App.
E

we establish the correctness of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\circ}}
.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}{x}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={x}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}{x}[i]{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={x}[i]\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\mathtt{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\mathtt{1}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\mathtt{1}^{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\mathtt{1}^{!}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\mathtt{fail}^{\widetilde{x}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\ B{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\lbag M\rbag^{!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\lbag M\rbag^{!}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\lbag M\rbag\cdot C{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\lbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\rbag\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}C{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}C\star U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}C{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\star{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}U\diamond V{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=U\diamond V\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\langle\!|}N/{x}{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\langle\!|}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}/{x}{|\!\rangle}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\llfloor{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}/{x}\rrceil\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\end{array}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\lambda x.M{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle=\lambda x.({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\langle{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{n}/{x}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{n}\leftarrow{x}])\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ $\#({x},M)=n$, each ${x}_{i}$ is fresh}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{C_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}C{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})}\hskip-14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\langle{\widetilde{x}}/{x}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\langle\!|}C_{i}(1)/{x}_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}C_{i}(k)/{x}_{k}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil,~\text{if $\#({x},M)=\mathsf{size}(C)=k$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M\langle{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{k}/{x}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}[{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{k}\leftarrow{x}]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small${\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}C\star U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle,~\text{if }\#({x},M)=k\geq 0\end{cases}

Figure 7:
Auxiliary Encoding:
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}

Example 25
.

We apply the encoding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}
in some of the
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
-terms from Example
3
: for simplicity, we assume that
N
and
U
have no free variables.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x)\ \lbag N\rbag\star U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\lambda x.x{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\lbag N\rbag\star U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=\lambda x.x_{1}[x_{1}\leftarrow x]\lbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\rbag\star{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x[1])\mathtt{1}\star\lbag N\rbag^{!}\diamond U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}(\lambda x.x[1]{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}\mathtt{1}\star\lbag N\rbag^{!}\diamond U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}=(\lambda x.x[1][\leftarrow x])\mathtt{1}\star\lbag{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\rbag^{!}\diamond{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}

4.4
Encoding
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi

We now define our encoding of
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
, and establish its correctness.

Notation 6
.

To help illustrate the behaviour of the encoding,
we use the names
x
,
x^{\ell}
, and
x^{!}
to denote three distinct channel names: while
x^{\ell}
is the channel that performs the linear substitution behaviour of the encoded term, channel
x^{!}
performs the unrestricted behaviour.

Definition 26

(From
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
: Expressions)
.

Let
u
be a name.
The encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}:u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\rightarrow\mathsf{s}\pi
is defined in Fig.
8
.

Every (free) variable
x
in an
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expression becomes a name
x
in its corresponding
\mathsf{s}\pi
process.
As customary in encodings of
\lambda
into
\pi
, we use a name
u
to provide the behaviour of the encoded expression.
In our case,
u
is a non-deterministic session: the encoded expression can be effectively available or not; this is signalled by prefixes
u.\overline{\mathtt{some}}

and

u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}
, respectively.

We discuss the most salient aspects of the encoding in Fig.
8
.

•

While linear variables are encoded as in
[
17
]
, the encoding of an unrestricted variable
x[j]
, not treated in
[
17
]
, is much more interesting: it first connects to a server along channel
x
via a request
\overline{x^{!}}?({x_{i}})
followed by a selection on
{x}_{i}.l_{j}
, which takes the
j
-th branch.

•

The encoding of
\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]
confirms its behaviour first followed by the receiving of a channel
x
. The channel
x
provides a linear channel
x^{\ell}
and an unrestricted channel
x^{!}
for dedicated substitutions of the linear and unrestricted bag components.

•

We encode
M\,(C\star U)
as a non-deterministic sum: an application involves a choice in the order in which the elements of
C
are substituted.

•

The encoding of
C\star U
synchronises with the encoding of
{\lambda x.M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]}
. The channel
x^{\ell}
provides the linear behaviour of the bag
C
while
x^{!}
provides the behaviour of
U
; this is done by guarding the encoding of
U
with a server connection such that every time a channel synchronises with
!x^{!}(x_{i})
a fresh copy of
U
is spawned.

•

The encoding of
{\lbag M\rbag\cdot\,C}
synchronises with the encoding of
{M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]}
, just discussed. The name
y_{i}
is used to trigger a failure in the computation if there is a lack of elements in the encoding of the bag.

•

The encoding of
{M[\widetilde{x}\leftarrow x]}
first confirms the availability of the linear behaviour along
x^{\ell}
. Then it sends a name
y_{i}
, which is used to collapse the process in the case of a failed reduction.
Subsequently, for each shared variable, the encoding receives a name, which will act as an occurrence of the shared variable.
At the end, a failure prefix on
x
is used to signal that there is no further information to send over.

•

The encoding of
U
synchronises with the last half encoding of
{x[j]}
; the name
x_{i}
selects the
j
-th term in the unrestricted bag.

•

The encoding of
{M{\langle\!|}N/x{|\!\rangle}}
is the composition of the encodings of
M
and
N
, where we await a confirmation of a behaviour along the variable that is being substituted.

•

{M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil}
is encoded as the composition of the encoding of
M
and a server guarding the encoding of
U
: in order for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
to gain access to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x_{i}}
it must first synchronise with the server channel
x^{!}
to spawn a fresh copy of
U
.

•

The encoding of
{\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}}
homomorphically preserves non-determinism.
Finally, the encoding of
\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}}
simply triggers failure on
u
and on each of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}
.

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt={x}.\overline{\mathtt{some}};[{x}\leftrightarrow u]\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}{x}[j]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=\overline{x^{!}}?({x_{i}}).{x}_{i}.l_{j};[{x_{i}}\leftrightarrow u]\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\lambda x.M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=u.\overline{\mathtt{some}};u(x).x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x^{\ell}).x(x^{!}).x.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{1.93748pt}{\small$C\star U$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=\!\!\displaystyle\bigoplus_{C_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(C)}\!\!(\nu x)(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x^{\ell}).x(x^{!}).x.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C_{i}\star U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x})\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M(C\star U){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{C_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(C)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{v}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C_{i}\star U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}))\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C\star U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}&\hskip-5.69054pt=x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{x}(x^{\ell}).\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x^{\ell}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\overline{x}(x^{!}).(!x^{!}(x_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x_{i}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\overline{\mathtt{close}})\big)\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}{\lbag M\rbag}\cdot{C}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x^{\ell}}&\hskip-5.69054pt=x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{lfv}(\lbag M\rbag\cdot C)};x^{\ell}(y_{i}).x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}_{y_{i},\mathsf{lfv}(\lbag M\rbag\cdot C)};x^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{some}};\overline{x^{\ell}}(x_{i}).\\[2.84526pt]
&\hskip 18.49988pt(x_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{lfv}(M)};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x_{i}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x^{\ell}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}y_{i}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}{\mathtt{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x^{\ell}}&\hskip-5.69054pt=x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x^{\ell}(y_{n}).(y_{n}.\overline{\mathtt{some}};y_{n}.\overline{\mathtt{close}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};x^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathtt{1}^{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}&\hskip-5.69054pt=x.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\lbag N\rbag^{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}&\hskip-5.69054pt={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}&\hskip-5.69054pt={x}.\mathtt{case}_{U_{i}\in U}\{\mathtt{l}_{i}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}U_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}\}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\langle\!|}N/{x}{|\!\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=(\nu{x})({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{x}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{lfv}(N)};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}N{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{{x}})\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M\llfloor U/{x}\rrceil{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=(\nu x^{!})({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}~!x^{!}(x_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x_{i}})\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M[\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=x^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x^{\ell}}(y_{i}).(y_{i}.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}(M)};y_{i}.\mathtt{close};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{none}})\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M[{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{n}\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=x^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x^{\ell}}(y_{1}).\big(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}.\mathtt{close};{\bf 0}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\\[2.84526pt]
&\hskip 18.49988ptx^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}_{u,(\mathsf{lfv}(M)\setminus{x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{n})};x^{\ell}({x}_{1}).{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M[{x}_{2},\cdots,{x}_{n}\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\big)\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\\[2.84526pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathtt{fail}^{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}&\hskip-5.69054pt=u.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x_{1}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}\cdots\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x_{k}.\overline{\mathtt{none}}\end{array}

Definition 28

(From
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
into
\mathsf{s}\pi
: Types)
.

The translation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}
in Figure
9
extends as follows to a context
\Gamma={x}_{1}{:}\sigma_{1},\cdots,{x}_{m}{:}\sigma_{m},{v}_{1}{:}\pi_{1},\cdots,{v}_{n}{:}\pi_{n}

and a context
\Theta=x^{!}_{1}{:}\eta_{1},\cdots,x^{!}_{k}{:}\eta_{k}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle={x}_{1}:{\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}},\cdots,{x}_{m}:{\binampersand}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\sigma_{m}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}},{v}_{1}:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\pi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{(\sigma,i_{1})}},\cdots,{v}_{n}:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\pi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{(\sigma,i_{n})}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=x^{!}_{1}:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\eta_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}},\cdots,x^{!}_{k}:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\eta_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}}

This encoding formally expresses how non-deterministic session protocols (typed with ‘
{\binampersand}
’) capture linear and unrestricted resource consumption in
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
.
Notice that the encoding of the multiset type
\pi
depends on two arguments (a strict type
\sigma
and a number
i\geq 0
) which are left unspecified above.
This is crucial to represent failures in
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
as typable processes in
\mathsf{s}\pi
.
For instance, given
(\sigma^{j},\eta)\rightarrow\tau
and
(\sigma^{k},\eta)
, the well-formedness rule for application admits a mismatch
(
j\neq k
, cf. Rule
\mathtt{[FS{:}app]}
in Fig.
14
, App.
D
).
In our proof of type preservation, the two arguments of the encoding are instantiated appropriately. Notice also how the client-server behaviour of unrestricted resources appears as ‘
!{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\eta{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}
’ in the encoding of the tuple type

(\sigma^{k},\eta)
.
With our encodings of expressions and types in place, we can now define our encoding of judgements:

Definition 29
.

If
\mathbb{M}
is an
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
expression such that

\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau

then we define the encoding of the judgement to be:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket},u:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}
.

The correctness of our encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}:\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}\to\mathsf{s}\pi
, stated in Theorem
31

(and detailed in App.
F
),
relies on a notion of
success
for both
\mathit{u}\lambda^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and
\mathsf{s}\pi
, given by the
\checkmark
construct:

Definition 30
.

We extend the syntax of terms for
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
and processes for
\mathsf{s}\pi
with
\checkmark
:

•

(In
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
)

\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
iff
there exist
M_{1},\cdots,M_{k}
such that

\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow^{*}M_{1}+\cdots+M_{k}
and

\mathsf{head}(M_{j}^{\prime})=\checkmark
, for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and term
M_{j}^{\prime}
such that
M_{j}\equiv_{\lambda}M_{j}^{\prime}
.

•

(In
\mathsf{s}\pi
)

P\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
holds whenever there exists a
P^{\prime}

such that

P\longrightarrow^{*}P^{\prime}

and
P^{\prime}
contains an unguarded occurrence of
\checkmark

(i.e., an occurrence that does not occur behind a prefix).

We now state operational correctness.
Fig.
10
illustrates the relation between completeness and soundness that the encoding satisfies: solid arrows denote reductions assumed, dashed arrows denote the application of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
, and dotted arrows denote the existing reductions that can be implied from the results.

We remark that since
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
satisfies the diamond property, it suffices to consider completeness based on a single reduction (
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
). Soundness uses the congruence
\equiv_{\lambda}
in Def.
23
.
We write
N\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}N^{\prime}
iff
N\equiv_{\lambda}N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{2}\equiv_{\lambda}N^{\prime}
, for some
N_{1},N_{2}
. Then,
\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}^{*}
is the reflexive, transitive closure of
\longrightarrow_{\equiv_{\lambda}}
. For success sensitivity, we decree
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\checkmark{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}=\checkmark
. We have:

Operational Completeness
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
:
\mathbb{N}
\mathbb{M}
\mathsf{s}\pi
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
Q\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
*
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
Thm
31
(b)
Operational Soundness
\mathbb{N}
\mathbb{N^{\prime}}
*
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
Q
Q^{\prime}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}

(c)

*
\equiv_{\lambda}

Figure 10:
An overview of operational soundness and completeness for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
.

Theorem 31

(Operational Correctness)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{M}
be well-formed
u\widehat{\lambda}^{\lightning}_{\oplus}
closed expressions.

(a)

(Type Preservation) Let
B
be a bag. We have:

(i)

If
\Theta;\Gamma\models B:(\sigma^{k},\eta)

then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}B{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\models{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket},u:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}(\sigma^{k},\eta){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{(\sigma,i)};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}
.

(ii)

If
\Theta;\Gamma\models\mathbb{M}:\tau

then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\models{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket},u:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\tau{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}
.

(b)

(Completeness) If
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}
then there exists
Q
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}Q\equiv_{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
.

(c)

(Soundness) If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\longrightarrow^{*}Q

then

Q\longrightarrow^{*}Q^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow^{*}_{\equiv_{\lambda}}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\equiv Q^{\prime}
,
for some
Q^{\prime},\mathbb{N}^{\prime}
.

(d)

(Success Sensitivity)
\mathbb{M}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
if, and only if,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\Downarrow_{\checkmark}
.

Proof.

Below we illustrate the most interesting case of the proof of soundness. Detailed proof can be found in App.
F
.
∎

Proof.

All items are proven by structural induction; a detailed proof can be
found in App.
F
.
Below we present the most interesting case in the proof of
soundness
: the case when
\mathbb{N}=M(C\star U)
. Then,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M(C\star U){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}=\bigoplus_{C_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(C)}(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{v}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C_{i}\star U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x})).

The proof then proceeds by induction on the number of reduction steps
k
that can be taken from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
, i.e,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\longrightarrow^{k}Q
. We will consider the case when
k\geq 1
, where for some process
R
and non-negative integers
n,m
such that
k=n+m
, we have the following:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{m}\bigoplus_{C_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(C)}(\nu v)(R\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}(C)};\overline{v}(x).([v\leftrightarrow u]\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C_{i}\star U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x}))\longrightarrow^{n}Q

There are several cases to analyse depending on the values of
m
and
n
, and the shape of
M
. We consider
m=0
,
n\geq 1
and
M=(\lambda x.(M^{\prime}[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}])){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U_{1}/{z_{1}}\rrceil\cdots\llfloor U_{q}/{z_{q}}\rrceil
, where
p,q\geq 0
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}
can perform the following reduction:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{*}\bigoplus_{C_{i}\in\mathsf{PER}(C)}(\nu\widetilde{y},\widetilde{z},x)(x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x^{\ell}).x(x^{!}).x[\,];{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}M^{\prime}[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}Q^{\prime\prime}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}C_{i}\star U{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{x})~(:=Q_{3})

where
Q^{\prime\prime}
defines the encoding of explicit substitutions within the encoded subterm
M
.
Notice that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=(\lambda x.(M^{\prime}[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}])){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U_{1}/{z_{1}}\rrceil\cdots\llfloor U_{q}/{z_{q}}\rrceil(C\star U)

\displaystyle\equiv_{\lambda}(\lambda x.(M^{\prime}[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}])(C\star U)){\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U_{1}/{z_{1}}\rrceil\cdots\llfloor U_{q}/{z_{q}}\rrceil

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M^{\prime}[{\widetilde{x}}\leftarrow{x}]\langle\!\langle\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$(C\star U)$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\rangle\!\rangle{\langle\!|}N_{1}/y_{1}{|\!\rangle}\cdots{\langle\!|}N_{p}/y_{p}{|\!\rangle}\llfloor U_{1}/{z_{1}}\rrceil\cdots\llfloor U_{q}/{z_{q}}\rrceil=\mathbb{M}

where the congruence holds assuming the necessary
\alpha
-renaming of variables.
Finally, one can verify that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\mathbb{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}_{u}=Q_{3}
, and the result follows.
∎

Example 32
.

Recall again term
M_{1}
from Example
3
.
It can be shown that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\longrightarrow^{*}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}\llfloor{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}U{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}/x^{!}\rrceil
.
To illustrate operational completeness, we can verify preservation of reduction, via
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}
: reductions below use the rules for
\mathsf{s}\pi
in Figure
5
—see Figure
11
.

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\displaystyle\llbracket}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}M_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rrbracket}=

\displaystyle(\nu v)({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v.\overline{\mathtt{some}};v(x).}x.\overline{\mathtt{some}};x(x^{\ell}).x(x^{!}).x[\,];x^{\ell}.\overline{\mathtt{some}}.\overline{x^{\ell}}(y_{1}).(y_{1}.\mathtt{some}_{\emptyset};y_{1}[\,];{\bf 0}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\displaystyle v.\mathtt{some}_{u,\mathsf{lfv}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})};\overline{v}(x).}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}[v\leftrightarrow u]}\mathord{\;\mathbf{|}\;}x.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{lfv}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})};\overline{x}(x^{\ell}).(x^{\ell}.\mathtt{some}_{\mathsf{lfv}({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\llparenthesis}N{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rrparenthesis^{\bullet}})};x^{\ell}(y_{1}).
